n\in\mathbb{N}
)
and
X^{*}
is an
q^{\prime}\times m
matrix and
q^{\prime}=O(q)
.
Moreover, without loss of generality, we assume in the following discussion that the corresponding matrix
X
is of dimension
q^{\prime}\times n
and work conditionally on the projection
\Pi_{n}
.

B.2
Reduction to uniformly bounded iid components

Note that arguments in this section do not depend on the projection matrix
\Pi_{n}
.
We now show that without loss of generality, we may assume that the random variables
X_{ij}
are centered, standardized and bounded.
To this aim, we will prove in what follows that

\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}^{*}d_{L}^{2}\left(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}}\right)\longrightarrow 0\ \ \text{in probability}

(B.3)

as
K\rightarrow\infty
, where
\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}=m^{-1}L_{n}\check{X}^{*}\check{X}^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top}
is built from the truncated, centered and standardized random variables

\check{X}_{ij}=\check{X}_{ij}(K)=\frac{X_{ij}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{jk}\arrowvert\leq K\}-\mathbb{E}X_{ij}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{jk}\arrowvert\leq K\}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(X_{ij}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{jk}\arrowvert\leq K\})}}

for an arbitrary constant
K>0
.
This is sufficient as it will turn out that the weak limit of
\mu^{\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}}
in probability does not depend on
K
.
Define the matrices
\tilde{X}=\tilde{X}(K)
and

\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}=m^{-1}L_{n}\tilde{X}^{*}\tilde{X}^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top}
, where

\tilde{X}_{ij}=X_{ij}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{jk}\arrowvert\leq K\}.

Next, define for any
\delta>0
the event

\Delta_{j,p,n}=\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\Big\arrowvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{ij}^{2}-\mathbb{E}X_{ij}^{2}\Big\arrowvert\vee\frac{1}{n}\Big\arrowvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(X_{ij}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{ij}\arrowvert>K\}-\mathbb{E}X_{ij}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{ij}\arrowvert>K\}\Big)\Big\arrowvert<\delta\Big\}.

With this notation, we introduce the Hermitian matrices

\hat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{m}L_{n}{X^{*}}^{\prime}{X^{*}}^{{}^{\prime}\top}L_{n}^{\top}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \Sigma_{n}^{*^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{m}L_{n}\tilde{X^{*}}^{\prime}{\tilde{X^{*}}}^{{}^{\prime}\top}L_{n}^{\top}

with

X^{\prime}_{ij}=X_{ij}\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}}\ \text{and}\ \tilde{X}^{\prime}_{ij}=\tilde{X}_{ij}\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}}.

Then

\displaystyle d_{L}

\displaystyle\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}}\Big)\leq d_{L}\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\hat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}}\Big)+d_{L}\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}},\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}}\Big)+d_{L}\Big(\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}},\mu^{\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}}\Big)

(B.4)

For the second term in (
B.4
),
we have by Theorem A.38 in
Bai and Silverstein, (2010)
, the Lidskii-Wielandt perturbation bound (1.2) in
Li and Mathias, (1999)
,

\limsup_{n}\|L_{n}\|_{S_{\infty}}\leq\sup_{n}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}
by the Representative Subpopulation Condition
3.1
, Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms, and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}d_{L}^{2}

\displaystyle\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}},\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{*}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q}\Big\arrowvert\lambda_{i}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}})-\lambda_{i}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}})\Big\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{*}\frac{1}{q}\big\Arrowvert\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{*}\frac{1}{qm}\Big\Arrowvert X^{*^{\prime}}X^{*^{\prime}\top}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}}\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}\top}\Big\Arrowvert_{S_{1}}\Big(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}+o(1)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{*}\frac{1}{qm}\Big\Arrowvert(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})^{\top}+(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}\top}+\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}}(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})^{\top}\Big\Arrowvert_{S_{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt\cdot\Big(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}+o(1)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{*}\frac{1}{qm}\bigg(\Big\Arrowvert(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})^{\top}\Big\Arrowvert_{S_{1}}+2\big\Arrowvert X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{2}}\big\Arrowvert\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt\cdot\Big(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}+o(1)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{qm}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}^{*}\tr\big((X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})^{\top}\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+2\Big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\tr\big((X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})^{\top}\big)\Big)^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\tr\big(\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}}\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}\top}\big)^{1/2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt\cdot\Big(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}+o(1)\Big).

But with
K^{\prime}>0
being a uniform upper bound on
q^{\prime}/q
we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{p}\frac{1}{qm}\mathbb{E}^{*}\tr\big((X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})(X^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}})^{\top}\big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{p}\frac{1}{qm}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(X_{ij}^{*^{\prime}}-\tilde{X}_{ij}^{*^{\prime}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{p}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q^{\prime}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(X_{ij}^{\prime}-\tilde{X}_{ij}^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{p}\frac{1}{qn}\sum_{j=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{ij}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{ij}\arrowvert>K\}

\displaystyle\leq K^{\prime}\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>K\big\}+\delta\big),

while

\displaystyle\sup_{p}\frac{1}{qm}\mathbb{E}^{*}\tr\big(\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}}\tilde{X}^{*^{\prime}\top}\big)

\displaystyle=\sup_{p}\frac{1}{qn}\sum_{j=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{ij}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{ij}\arrowvert\leq K\}\leq K^{\prime}\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}+2\delta\big).

Summarizing these calculations, we obtain for the second term in (
B.4
) the estimate

\displaystyle\limsup_{p\to\infty}\mathbb{E}^{*}d_{L}^{2}\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}},\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}}\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\ K^{\prime}\sup_{n}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}\Big\{\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>K\big\}+\delta\big)

\displaystyle\ \ +2\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>K\big\}+\delta\big)^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}+2\delta\big)^{1/2}\Big\}.

For a corresponding estimate of the first term in (
B.4
) we note that
\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{j,p,n})\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
by the weak law of large numbers. Moreover, for fixed
n
, the value
\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{j,p,n})
is the same for all
j\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
. Hence, for sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}^{c}}\geq\delta q\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{q^{\prime}}\big(\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}^{c}}-\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{j,p,n}^{c})\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta q\Big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{\delta^{2}q}{2K^{\prime}}\Big)

by Hoeffding’s inequality. The Borel-Cantelli lemma then reveals

\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{q^{\prime}}\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}^{c}}<\delta

almost surely (with the exceptional set not depending on the sequence of
\Pi_{n}^{\prime}
s). Using that
d_{L}\leq d_{K}
, where
d_{K}
denotes the Kolmogorov distance, Theorem A.43 of
Bai and Silverstein, (2010)
, the inequality
\rank(AB)\leq\min(\rank(A),\rank(B))
we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}d_{L}\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\hat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}d_{K}\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\hat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{q}\rank\Big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{q}\rank\Big(X^{*}X^{*\top}-X^{*^{\prime}}X^{*^{\prime}\top}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{q}\sharp\Big\{j\in\{1,\dots,q^{\prime}\}:\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}^{c}}=1\Big\}\leq\delta~~~\text{a.s.}

Here the fourth inequality follows from

\displaystyle\text{rank}\Big(X^{*}X^{*^{\prime}}-X^{*^{\prime}}X^{*^{\prime}\top}\Big)

\displaystyle=\text{rank}\Big(\sum^{m}_{i=1}(X^{*}_{i}X^{*\top}_{i}-X_{i}^{*^{\prime}}X_{i}^{*^{\prime}\top})\Big)

\displaystyle=\text{rank}\Big(\sum^{n}_{i=1}\delta_{i}\big({X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}-{X_{i}^{\prime}}X_{i}^{{}^{\prime}\top}}\big)\Big)

where
\delta_{i}\in\sharp\{j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}~|~X_{j}^{*}=X_{i}\}\in\{0,\ldots,m\}

with
\sum^{n}_{i=1},\delta_{i}=m
and the fact that the
j
the row and column of the
q^{\prime}\times q^{\prime}
matrix
\sum^{n}_{i=1}\delta_{i}(X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}-X_{i}X_{i}^{{}^{\prime}\top})
are the
0
-vector if
\mathds{1}_{\Delta_{j,p,n}^{c}}=0
.

The third term in (
B.4
) can be bounded by
\delta>0
analogously. Summarizing the estimates for the terms on the right-hand side of (
B.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{E}^{*}d_{L}^{2}\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle 2\delta+\Big[K^{\prime}\sup_{n}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>K\big\}+\delta\big)

\displaystyle+2K^{\prime}\sup_{n}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>K\big\}+\delta\big)^{1/2}\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}+2\delta\big)^{1/2}\Big]^{1/2}.

almost surely. Since
\delta>0
may be chosen arbitrarily small,
it follows that

\displaystyle\limsup_{p\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}^{*}d_{L}^{2}\Big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*K}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq K^{\prime}\sup_{n}\Arrowvert A_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}\Big[\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>K\big\}+2\big(\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{2}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>K\big\}\big)^{1/2}\Big]

almost surely. Now, the last expression can be made arbitrarily small for
K
sufficiently large, independently of the projection
\Pi_{n}
. Since the centralization of the truncated random variables
\tilde{X}_{ij}
leads to a finite rank perturbation of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}
(uniformly in
p
), we may assume the entries
\tilde{X}_{ij}
to be centered. Next, as in the truncation step by replacing there
\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{ij}\arrowvert\leq K\}
with

1/\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(X_{ij}\mathds{1}\{\arrowvert X_{jk}\arrowvert\leq K\})}

in the definition of
\tilde{X}
, we may assume the entries to be standardized since the variance of the truncated variables converges to one as the truncation level tends to infinity, which
completes the proof of (
B.3
).

Note that the matrix
L_{n}
can be a random matrix, but it is independent of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
as well as from
X_{1}^{*},\dots,X_{m}^{*}
. As a a consequence of Section
B.1
and
B.2
,
we assume from now on that conditional on
\Pi_{n}
, the variables
X_{ij}
are centered, standardized and bounded, that the vectors
X_{i}
have
q^{\prime}=O(q)
components and that the matrix
L_{n}
is of dimension
q\times q^{\prime}
.

B.3
A first non-standard result on quadratic forms

In this section, we derive moment bounds on

X_{1}^{*\top}C^{*}(X_{2}^{*},\dots,X_{m}^{*})X_{1}^{*}-\tr C^{*}(X_{2}^{*},\dots,X_{m}^{*})

for
the particular matrices

\displaystyle C^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{n}^{\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}L_{n},

(B.5)

\displaystyle C^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{n}^{\prime}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}L_{n}~.

(B.6)

Proposition B.1
.

For any
p\geq 2
, there exists some constant
K_{p}(z)>0
, such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{*\top}C^{*}X_{1}^{*}-\tr C^{*}\big\arrowvert^{p}\leq{K_{p}(z)}\Big(m^{p/2}+\frac{m^{p+1}}{n}\Big),

(B.7)

where
C^{*}
is either given by (
B.6
) or (
B.5
) and the constant
K_{p}(z)

depends only
z
and
p
.

A natural idea of proving this result is to
first
condition on
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and applying
Lemma B.26 of
Bai and Silverstein, (2010)
to

\mathbb{E}_{X}^{*}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{*\top}C^{*}X_{1}^{*}-\tr C^{*}\big\arrowvert^{p}.

However, this approach fails as the components
of the vector
X_{1}^{*}
are conditional on
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
neither independent nor normalized.
Therefore, a proof of the estimate
(
B.7
) for the unconditional expectation relies on a different argument, which originates the condition
m=o(n)
. Note that in the unconditional world, the vector
X_{1}^{*}
and the matrix
C^{*}
are not independent any longer.

Since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}^{*}\big[\arrowvert{X_{1}^{*}}^{\top}C^{*}X_{1}^{*}-\tr C^{*}\arrowvert^{p}~\big|X_{2}^{*},\ldots,X_{m}^{*}\big]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\arrowvert X_{j}^{\top}C^{*}X_{j}-\tr C^{*}\arrowvert^{p}\Big]

(B.8)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}C^{*}X_{1}-\tr C^{*}\arrowvert^{p},

it is sufficient to deduce the bound for the right-hand side, that is

\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}C^{*}X_{1}-\tr C^{*}\arrowvert^{p}\leq K_{p}(z)\Big(m^{p/2}+\frac{m^{p+1}}{n}\Big)\ .

Because of
X_{1}^{*},\dots,X_{m}^{*}\overset{iid}{\sim}\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{n}
, the matrix
C^{*}
in (
B.17
) depends on
X_{1}
and therefore, standard results results on centered random quadratic forms as Lemma B.26 in
Bai and Silverstein, (2010)
are not directly applicable.

For
i=1,\dots,m
define

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{i}^{*}

\displaystyle=X_{i}^{*}I\{X_{i}^{*}\not=X_{1}\},\ \ \tilde{r}_{i}^{*}={1\over\sqrt{m}}L_{n}\tilde{X}_{i}^{*},

(B.9)

and write

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}^{*}=\sharp\big\{i\in\{2,\dots,m\}:X_{i}^{*}=X_{1}\big\}~,~~\bar{\Delta}_{n}^{*}=\sharp\big\{i\in\{1,\dots,m\}:X_{i}^{*}=X_{1}\big\}.

(B.10)

We note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}[\Delta_{n}^{*}]={m-1\over n}~,~~\mathbb{E}^{*}[\bar{\Delta}_{n}^{*}]={m\over n}.

(B.11)

For any matrix
A
built from
X_{1}^{*},\dots,X_{m}^{*}
, we write
\tilde{A}
for the corresponding matrix which arises by replacing
X_{i}^{*}
by
\tilde{X}_{i}^{*}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, in the definition of
A
. Furthermore, we introduce

\displaystyle C^{*}=L_{n}^{\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}BL_{n}

(B.12)

\displaystyle\check{C}^{*}=L_{n}^{\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\tilde{B}L_{n}

(B.13)

where

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}

(B.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{B}

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}

(B.15)

and

\widetilde{\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}=\mathbb{E}^{*}{q\over m}\Big\{{1\over q}{\rm tr}\Big[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{r}_{j}^{*}\tilde{r}_{j}^{*\top}-zI_{q}\Big]^{-1}\Big\}-{1-q/m\over z}~.

Note that the difference between the terms

\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)

and
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}

consists in the fact that the sum in the
latter term does not contain the variable

X_{1}
anymore.
In a first step, we replace
C^{*}
by
\check{C}^{*}

with an error of order
O(m/n)
. For this purpose,
we use the identity
A^{-1}_{1}-A_{2}^{-1}=A^{-1}_{1}(A_{2}-A_{1})A_{2}^{-1}
together with the Sherman-Morrison formula to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}(C^{*}-\check{C}^{*})X_{1}\big\arrowvert^{p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big|X_{1}^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\mathbb{E}^{*}\Big[{1\over m^{2}}\frac{\bar{\Delta}_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\tilde{D}^{*}(z)^{-2}L_{n}X_{1}}{1+\frac{1}{m}X_{1}^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\tilde{D}^{*}(z)^{-1}L_{n}X_{1}}\Big]\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}L_{n}X_{1}\Big|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq{1\over m^{2p}}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X_{1}^{\top}X_{1}|^{2p}(\bar{\Delta}_{n}^{*})^{p}\|L_{n}\|_{S_{\infty}}^{6p}\max\Big(\frac{4\Arrowvert L_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}}{\Im(z)},2\Big)^{2p}\frac{{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}^{p}}{\Im(z)^{4p}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq K_{p}(z){m\over n},

where we have used Lemma 2.3

Silverstein, (1995)
together with
\Arrowvert\tilde{D}^{*}(z)^{-1}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}\leq 1/\Im(z)
and
\arrowvert 1/(1+\frac{1}{m}X_{1}^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\tilde{D}^{*}(z)^{-1}L_{n}X_{1})\arrowvert\leq\arrowvert z\arrowvert/\Im(z)
for the first inequality, (
B.11
), and the fact that the
X_{i}
and
X_{i}^{*}
are

q´=O(q)=O(m)
dimensional vectors
with uniformly bounded components
(by the arguments in Section
B.1

and
B.2
).
Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert{\rm tr}(C^{*}-\check{C}^{*})\big\arrowvert^{p}\leq K_{p}(z){m\over n}.

and it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}C^{*}X_{1}-\tr C^{*}-\big(X_{1}^{\top}\check{C}^{*}X_{1}-\tr\check{C}^{*}\big)\big\arrowvert^{p}\leq K_{p}(z){m\over n}.

(B.16)

Consequently, it is sufficient to show the assertion for the matrix

\displaystyle C^{*}=L_{n}^{\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}ML_{n}~,

(B.17)

where
M
a potentially random matrix, which is, conditionally on
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
independent of
X_{1}^{*}
, depending only on
X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}

with almost surely bounded spectral norm
(uniformly in
n
). We then apply the result for the matrices

M=I_{q}
and
M=\tilde{B}
in (
B.15
) (note the latter has a uniformly bounded spectral norm by Lemma 2.3 in

Silverstein, (1995)
).

By the Sherman-Morrison formula,

\displaystyle C^{*}

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}^{*}-\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}L_{n}^{\top}\tilde{D}_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}{1\over\sqrt{m}}L_{n}X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}{1\over\sqrt{m}}L_{n}^{\top}\tilde{D}_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}L_{n}}{1+{1\over m}\Delta_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\tilde{D}_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}L_{n}X_{1}}\cdot M

(B.18)

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}^{*}-\frac{{1\over{m}}\Delta_{n}^{*}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}}{1+{1\over m}\Delta_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}\cdot M

where
\tilde{D}_{1}^{*}
is defined as
D_{1}^{*}
with
X_{2}^{*},\dots,X_{m}^{*}
replaced by
\tilde{X}_{2}^{*},\dots,\tilde{X}_{m}^{*}

and

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{*}=\tilde{C}(\tilde{X}_{2}^{*},\dots,\tilde{X}_{m}^{*})=L_{n}^{\top}\tilde{D}_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}L_{n}\cdot M.

(B.19)

Note that the matrix
\tilde{C}^{*}
does not depend on the random variable
X_{1}
anymore. Therefore, inserting the conditional expectation with respect to
\tilde{X}_{2}^{*},\dots,\tilde{X}_{m}^{*}
, Lemma B.26 in
Bai and Silverstein, (2010)
reveals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}-\tr\tilde{C}^{*}\big\arrowvert^{p}\leq K_{p}(z)m^{p/2}~.

(B.20)

Consequently, it remains to derive a bound
for the difference

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{*}-C^{*}=\frac{{1\over{m}}\Delta_{n}^{*}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}}{1+{1\over m}\Delta_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}\cdot M,

(B.21)

that is, a bound on

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}(\tilde{C}^{*}-C^{*})X_{1}-\tr(\tilde{C}^{*}-C^{*})\big\arrowvert^{p}.

(B.22)

Employing the estimate (3.4) in

Bai and Silverstein, (1998)

for the denominator yields

\displaystyle\bigg\arrowvert\frac{\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}{1+\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}\bigg\arrowvert

\displaystyle=\bigg\arrowvert\frac{\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}{1+\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}\bigg\arrowvert I_{\big\{\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}\arrowvert>2\big\}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\bigg\arrowvert\frac{\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}{1+\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}\bigg\arrowvert I_{\big\{\frac{\Delta_{n}^{*}}{m}\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}\arrowvert\leq 2\big\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(1+\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)}\Big),

(B.23)

and we obtain (using (
B.21
) twice)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\arrowvert

\displaystyle X_{1}^{\top}(C^{*}-\tilde{C}^{*})X_{1}-\tr(C^{*}-\tilde{C}^{*})\Big\arrowvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}(C^{*}-\tilde{C}^{*})X_{1}\big\arrowvert^{p}+2^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert\tr(C^{*}-\tilde{C}^{*})\big\arrowvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}\bigg(1+\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)}\bigg)^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\arrowvert{1\over m}\Delta_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}\arrowvert}{|1+{1\over m}\Delta_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}|}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}MX_{1}\big\arrowvert^{p}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+2^{p-1}{1\over m^{p}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert\Delta_{n}^{*}\frac{X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}M\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}{1+{1\over m}\Delta_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}}\bigg\arrowvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}\bigg(1+\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)}\bigg)^{p-1}\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)}{1\over m}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Delta_{n}^{*}\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}\big\arrowvert\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}MX_{1}\big\arrowvert^{p}\Big]

(B.24)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+2^{p-1}{|z|^{p}\over\Im^{p}(z)}{1\over m^{p}}\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert\Delta_{n}^{*}X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}M\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}\big\arrowvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2p-2}\bigg(1+\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)}\bigg)^{p-1}\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)}{1\over m}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Delta_{n}^{*}E_{X_{1}}\big[\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}\big\arrowvert\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}MX_{1}\big\arrowvert^{p}\big]\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+2^{p-1}{|z|^{p}\over\Im^{p}(z)}{1\over m^{p}}\mathbb{E}\Big[(\Delta_{n}^{*})^{p}E_{X_{1}}\big[\big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}\tilde{C}^{*}M\tilde{C}^{*}X_{1}\big\arrowvert^{p}\big]\Big]

\displaystyle\leq K_{p}(z)\Big(\frac{m^{p+1}}{n}\Big),

(B.25)

where we used the fact that
X_{1}
has uniformly bounded components and that the spectral norms of
\tilde{C}^{*}
and
M
are also uniformly bounded.
Combining this result with (
B.20
) completes the proof.
∎

B.4
Remaining part of the proof

Let
\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}}
denote the spectral measure of the matrix
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}
and denote by

m_{n}^{*}:\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}

the corresponding Stieltjes transform,
that is

\displaystyle m_{n}^{*}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\tr\Big[\big(\hat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}-zI_{q}\big)^{-1}\Big]

(B.26)

We define

\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)=\frac{q}{m}m_{n}^{*}(z)-\Big(1-\frac{q}{m}\Big)\frac{1}{z}

and denote by

\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}
the spectral distribution of the matrix
\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}
defined in (
B.2
).
Finally, we define

\displaystyle\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}=\underline{m}_{\frac{p}{n},\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}}^{0}

(B.27)

as the solution of the equation (
2.5
) with
G=\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}
and
\gamma=p/n
.
In order to show

\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}}-\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}}\Longrightarrow 0\ \ \text{in probability}

(B.28)

we will
prove in Subsection
B.5
and
B.6
that, conditionally on
\Pi_{n}
,

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)-\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\big\arrowvert

\displaystyle=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\frac{m}{n}\Big)~,

(B.29)

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)-\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\big\arrowvert

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\Big)~.

(B.30)

As a consequence of the previous two steps,

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)-\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\big\arrowvert={o}_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(B.31)

for any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
conditionally on
\Pi_{n}
. Note that both terms in this expression depend on the random projection
\Pi_{n}
.

Due to condition (
3.1
) in the Representative Subpopulation Condition, we have

\displaystyle|\underline{m}_{\frac{p}{n},\mu^{\Sigma_{n}}}^{0}(z)-\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\big|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(B.32)

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, because the solution of the MP-equation (
2.4
) is continuous in
H
(with respect to the topology of weak convergence); see formula (3.10) and the discussion in the lines below in
Bai and Silverstein, (1998)
.
Therefore,
(see equation (
2.5
) with
\gamma=p/n
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)-\underline{m}_{\frac{p}{n},\mu^{\Sigma_{n}}}^{0}(z)\big\arrowvert=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)~.

(B.33)

Let
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{+}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\dots\}
be a countable dense subset of
\mathbb{C}^{+}
and denote by
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
an arbitrary subsequence of
(n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Due to the characterization of stochastic convergence in terms of almost sure convergence, there exists some subsubsequence
(k_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\big\arrowvert\underline{m}_{k_{n}^{\prime}}^{*}(z_{1})-\underline{m}^{0}_{\mu^{\Sigma_{k_{n}^{\prime}}}}(z_{1})\big\arrowvert\rightarrow 0\ \text{a.s.}

where here and subsequently, the dependence on
\gamma={p(k_{n}^{\prime})/k_{n}^{\prime}}
is suppressed.
Denote the exceptional null set by
N_{1}\subset\Omega
. Due to (
B.28
) again, there exists a subsequence
(k_{n}^{\prime\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(k_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\big\arrowvert\underline{m}_{k_{n}^{\prime\prime}}^{*}(z_{2})-\underline{m}^{0}_{\mu^{\Sigma_{k_{n}^{\prime\prime}}}}(z_{2})\big\arrowvert\rightarrow 0

outside a null set
N_{2}
. Continuing inductively and applying finally the Cantor diagonalization principle, we extract a subsequence
(\tilde{k}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\big\arrowvert\underline{m}_{\tilde{k}_{n}}^{*}(z)-\underline{m}^{0}_{\mu^{\Sigma_{\tilde{k}_{n}}}}(z)\big\arrowvert\rightarrow 0\ \ \forall\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{0}^{+}

outside the null set
N=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}N_{j}
. For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{C}_{\ell}^{+}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:\Im(z)>1/\ell,\arrowvert z\arrowvert\leq\ell\}
. Then
\arrowvert\underline{m}^{0}_{\mu^{\Sigma_{\tilde{k}_{n}}}}(z)\arrowvert\leq l
and
|\underline{m}_{\tilde{k}_{n}}^{*}(z)\arrowvert\leq l
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}_{l}^{+}
. By Vitali’s convergence theorem,

\big\arrowvert\underline{m}_{\tilde{k}_{n}}^{*}(z)-\underline{m}^{0}_{\mu^{\Sigma_{\tilde{k}_{n}}}}(z)\big\arrowvert\rightarrow 0\ \ \forall\,z\in\mathbb{C}_{l}^{+}\ \ \text{a.s.}

As this convergence is true for every
l\in\mathbb{N}
, we conclude

\big\arrowvert\underline{m}_{\tilde{k}_{n}}^{*}(z)-\underline{m}^{0}_{\mu^{\Sigma_{\tilde{k}_{n}}}}(z)\big\arrowvert\rightarrow 0\ \ \forall\,z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\ \ \text{a.s.}

But as
\underline{m}^{0}_{{p\over n},\mu^{\Sigma_{n}}}(z)\rightarrow\underline{m}_{c,H}^{0}(z)

\forall\,z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
and

m_{c,H}^{0}
is the Stieltjes transform of a probability
measure
\mu_{c,H}^{0}
with compact support, this implies
weak convergence of

(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tilde{k}_{n}}^{*}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Longrightarrow\mu_{c,H}^{0}

almost surely. Since
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
was an arbitrary subsequence,

\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}}\Rightarrow\mu_{c,H}^{0}\ \ \text{in probability.}

Finally, by the triangle inequality,
Lemma
A.1
and
\mu^{0}_{{p\over n},\Sigma_{n}}\Rightarrow\mu_{c,H}^{0}
, we have

d_{BL}\big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\check{k}_{n}}^{*}},\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tilde{k}_{n}}}\big)\longrightarrow 0\ \ \text{a.s.}

and therefore

d_{BL}\big(\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}},\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}}\big)\longrightarrow 0

in probability.

B.5
Proof of (
B.29
)

Recall the definition of the population
covariance matrix
\Sigma_{n}
and that the matrix

\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}=\Pi_{n}\Sigma_{n}\Pi_{n}^{\top}=L_{n}L_{n}^{\top}

in (
B.2
) is the population
covariance matrix
corresponding to sub-sampling process, where
L_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q^{\prime}}
. Note
that
\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}
can be a random object which is independent of

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
. With the notation from Section
A.1
we can rewrite
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}r_{j}^{*}r_{j}^{*\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}.

Next, we define (for
\ z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
) the Stieltjes transform

\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)=\frac{q}{m}m_{n}^{*}(z)-\Big(1-\frac{q}{m}\Big)\frac{1}{z}~,

where
{m}_{n}^{*}(z)
denotes the Stieltjes transform
of the spectral measure
\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}}

of the matrix
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}
defined in (
B.26
)
(note that the supports of the measures corresponding to
\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)
and
{m}_{n}^{*}(z)

differ by
\arrowvert m-q\arrowvert
zeros only).

As in expression (5.2) of

Bai and Silverstein, (1998)
, we obtain the identity

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{q}{m}&\int\frac{\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}+z\frac{q}{m}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(m_{n}^{*}(z)\big)\\
&=\mathbb{E}^{*}\bigg\{\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\bigg[r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}r_{1}^{*}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\bigg]\bigg\}.\end{split}

(B.34)

Rewriting the left-hand side

\displaystyle\frac{q}{m}

\displaystyle\int\frac{\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}+z\frac{q}{m}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(m_{n}^{*}(z)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{q}{m}\bigg(1-\int\frac{t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}{1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)\bigg)+z\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\big)+\Big(1-\frac{q}{m}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\big)\bigg[z-\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t}{1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)+\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\bigg]

and recalling that

z-\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t}{1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)}d\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)+\frac{1}{\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)}=0

(B.35)

by (
2.5
), we start with establishing the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}

\displaystyle\bigg\{\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\bigg[r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}r_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big((\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big)\bigg]\bigg\}\bigg\arrowvert

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{m}+\frac{m}{n}\Big).

(B.36)

This is carried out in the subsequent Steps (i) – (iii). Their proofs are given at the end of this paragraph.

(i)

We shall prove the bound

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert\tr

\displaystyle\Big(\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle-\tr\Big(\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big)\bigg\arrowvert=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1}).

(B.37)

(uniformly with respect to the projection
\Pi_{n}
).

(ii)

The next aim is to verify

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\Big\arrowvert\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big)\Big\arrowvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1}).

(B.38)

(iii)

We shall deduce (
B.36
).

Finally, we prove

\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert\geq c(z)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(m^{-1}).

(B.39)

for some constant
c(z)>0
. Because of the identity

\displaystyle z\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)=-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}^{*}(z)

(B.40)

(
Silverstein,, 1995
, cf. identity (2.2) in )
, we have
\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert=\arrowvert z\arrowvert^{-1}\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\arrowvert
. But it follows from (
B.52
) and the inequalities
\arrowvert b_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert,\arrowvert\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\arrowvert\leq\arrowvert z\arrowvert/\Im(z)
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\beta_{1}^{*}(z)-b_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(b_{n}^{*}(z)\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)\big)\Big\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert^{2}}{\big(\Im(z)\big)^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{1/2}_{X}\big\arrowvert\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)\big\arrowvert^{2}=\mathcal{O}\Big({1\over m^{1/2}}+\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}\Big),

(B.41)

while

\displaystyle\arrowvert b_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert\geq\frac{1}{1+\Arrowvert\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}/\Im(z)},

which is bounded away from zero uniformly
in
\Pi_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, (
B.39
) is verified.

Having established (
B.36
) and (
B.39
), we conclude that

\arrowvert\omega_{n}(z)\arrowvert=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(m^{-1}+m/n)

(B.42)

with

\displaystyle\omega_{n}(z)

\displaystyle:=z-\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t}{1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)+\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)},

(B.43)

that is,
\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)
is an approximate solution to the fixed point equation (
B.35
) for
\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}
. Next, we may rewrite
(
B.43
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)=-\bigg(z-\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t}{1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)+\omega_{n}(z)\bigg)^{-1}.

(B.44)

From (
B.44
) and the
equation (
B.35
)
we get the identity

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)-\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)=\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)-\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\big)\kappa_{n}(z)+\omega_{n}(z)\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)

(B.45)

with

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}(z)=\frac{q}{m}\frac{\int\frac{t^{2}}{(1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z))(1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{\Big(-z+\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t}{1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)-\omega_{n}(z)\Big)\Big(-z+\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t}{1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)\Big)}.

An application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the identity

\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))=\frac{\Im(z)+\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t^{2}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}}{\Big\arrowvert-z+\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t}{1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)\Big\arrowvert^{2}}

(which follows from (
2.5
)), and a similar identity for the second factor (which follows from (
B.43
))
yields

\displaystyle\arrowvert\kappa_{n}(z)\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq\left[\frac{\frac{q}{m}\Im(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z))\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{\Im(z)+\Im(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z))\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)+\Im(\omega_{n}(z))}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times\left[\frac{\frac{q}{m}\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{\Im(z)+\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}\right]^{1/2}~.

In the case
\arrowvert\Im(\omega_{n}(z))/\Im(z)\arrowvert<1
this in turn can be bounded by

\left[\frac{\frac{q}{m}\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{\Im(z)+\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}\right]^{1/2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\,\mathbb{P}\Big(\arrowvert\kappa_{n}(z)\arrowvert<1\big)

\displaystyle\ \ \geq\liminf_{n}\mathbb{P}\left(\left[\frac{\frac{q}{m}\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{\Im(z)+\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}\right]^{1/2}<1,\ \arrowvert\Im(\omega_{n}(z))/\Im(z)\arrowvert<1\right)

\displaystyle\ \ =1

because
\Im(\omega_{n}(z))\rightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0
and

\frac{\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}{\Im(z)+\Im(\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z))\frac{q}{m}\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}(t)}\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}\frac{\Im(\underline{m}_{0}(z))c\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{m}_{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd H(t)}{\Im(z)+\Im(\underline{m}_{0}(z))c\int\frac{t^{2}}{\arrowvert 1+t\underline{m}_{0}(z)\arrowvert^{2}}\dd{H}(t)}<1

(note that the support of
\mu^{\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}}
is uniformly bounded
because
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|L_{n}\|_{S_{\infty}}<\infty
, by assumption).
At the same time,
\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert
and
\arrowvert\underline{\tilde{m}}_{n}^{0}(z)\arrowvert
are bounded from above by
1/\Im(z)
.
Hence, (
B.29
) follows from
(
B.42
) and (
B.45
) .

•

Proof of (i).
By the Sherman-Morrison formula applied to the matrix
D^{*}(z)-D_{1}^{*}(z)
, we may rewrite the left-hand side of (
B.37
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert\tr\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\tr\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\bigg\arrowvert

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\Big(\beta_{1}^{*}(z)r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}r_{1}^{*}\Big)\bigg\arrowvert.

(B.46)

Next, as
\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(.)
is a Stieltjes transform and the class of Stieltjes transforms is closed under convex combination,
\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}
is a Stieltjes transform again, such that Lemma 2.3 in

Silverstein, (1995)
implies

\Big\Arrowvert(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\Big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}\leq\max\bigg(\frac{4\Arrowvert\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}}{\Im(z)},2\bigg).

(B.47)

Using additionally the estimates
\arrowvert\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\arrowvert\leq\arrowvert z\arrowvert/\Im(z)
,
\Arrowvert D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}\leq 1/\Im(z)
, we find that (
B.46
) can be bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{(\Im(z))^{3}}\max\bigg(\frac{4\Arrowvert\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{3}}{\Im(z)},2\Arrowvert\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}\bigg)\mathcal{O}(m^{-1}).

•

Proof of (ii).
Using the representation by a telecope sum and recalling the notation (
A.11
) yields

\displaystyle D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}-\mathbb{E}^{*}\big[D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{m}\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{m}\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)\Big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}-D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=2}^{m}\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)\Big(D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}r_{j}^{*}r_{j}^{*\top}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}\beta_{1j}^{*}(z)\Big)

and the fact that these
(m-1)
summands are orthogonal with respect to
\mathbb{E}^{*}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\Big\arrowvert\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big)\Big\arrowvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{m}\mathbb{E}\Big\arrowvert\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)\beta_{1j}^{*}(z)r_{j}^{*\top}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}r_{j}^{*}\Big\arrowvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{m^{3}}\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)}\mathbb{E}\Big\arrowvert X_{1}^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}D_{12}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q}\big)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{12}^{*}(z)^{-1}L_{n}X_{1}\Big\arrowvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{m^{3}}\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert}{\Im(z)^{5}}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{1}\|_{2}^{4}]\cdot\|L_{n}\|_{S_{\infty}}^{8}\max\Big(\frac{4\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\|_{S_{\infty}}}{\Im(z)},2\|\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\|_{S_{\infty}}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(m^{-1})

where we have used again Lemma 2.3 in

Silverstein, (1995)
.

•

Proof of (iii).
It follows from (i) that the left-hand side in (
B.36
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\bigg\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\bigg\{\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\bigg[r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}r_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Big)\bigg]\bigg\}\bigg\arrowvert+\mathcal{O}(m^{-1})

(B.48)

(uniformly with respect to
\Pi_{n}
).
Employing the identity

\displaystyle\beta_{1}^{*}(z)-b_{n}^{*}(z)

\displaystyle=-\beta_{1}^{*}(z)b_{n}^{*}(z)\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)=-b_{n}^{*}(z)^{2}\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)+b_{n}^{*}(z)^{2}\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)^{2}

(B.49)

with the definition of
\gamma_{1}^{*}
in (
A.15
) (note that
L_{n}L_{n}^{\top}=\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}
),
we may rewrite

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\bigg\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\bigg\{\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\bigg[r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}r_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Big)\bigg]\bigg\}\bigg\arrowvert

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\arrowvert b_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert^{2}\bigg\arrowvert\mathbb{E}^{*}\bigg\{\Big(\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)-\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)^{2}\Big)\bigg[r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}r_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Big)\bigg]\bigg\}\bigg\arrowvert\bigg].

(B.50)

Using the bounds
\arrowvert b_{n}^{*}(z)\arrowvert,\arrowvert\beta_{1}^{*}(z)\arrowvert\leq\arrowvert z\arrowvert/\Im(z)
, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (ii) shows that (
B.48
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{\arrowvert z\arrowvert^{2}}{\big(\Im(z)\big)^{2}}\bigg(2\mathbb{E}\arrowvert\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)\arrowvert^{2}+\frac{2\arrowvert z\arrowvert^{2}}{\big(\Im(z)\big)^{2}}\mathbb{E}\arrowvert\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)\arrowvert^{4}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\bigg[\mathbb{E}^{1/2}_{X}\bigg\arrowvert r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}r_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Big)\bigg\arrowvert^{2}+\mathcal{O}\big(m^{-1/2}\big)\bigg].

Note that, conditional on
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, the random variable
D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}
is independent of
r_{1}^{*}
. Hence,
by Proposition
B.1

(with the matrix
C^{*}
in (
B.6
)), we obtain for the second factor

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert r_{1}^{*\top}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}r_{1}^{*}-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}(\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}+I_{q})^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\Big)\bigg\arrowvert^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~=\mathcal{O}\Big({1\over m}+{m\over n}\Big).

(B.51)

In order to complete the proof of (iii), we continue to show

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\arrowvert\gamma_{1}^{*}(z)\arrowvert^{p}=\mathcal{O}\Big({1\over m^{p/2}}+\frac{m}{n}\Big)\ \ \ \text{for any $p\geq 2$}.

(B.52)

To this aim, note first that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big\arrowvert{r_{1}^{*\top}}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}r_{1}^{*}-\frac{1}{m}\tr\big(\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big\arrowvert^{p}\leq{K_{p}(z)}\Big(\frac{1}{m^{p/2}}+\frac{m}{n}\Big)

(B.53)

by Proposition
B.1
, where the constant depends only on
z
and
p
.
But the same consideration as at the beginning of step (ii) provides the identity

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\Big\arrowvert\frac{1}{m}\tr\big(\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)-\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big(\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}\mathbb{E}^{*}\big(D_{1}^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big)\Big\arrowvert^{p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=2}^{m}\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)\beta_{1j}^{*}(z){r_{j}^{*}\top}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}(z)r_{j}^{*}\bigg\arrowvert^{p},

which is bounded by the (discrete) Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, the inequality
\arrowvert\beta_{1j}^{*}(z)\arrowvert\leq\arrowvert z\arrowvert/\Im(z)
, Jensen’s inequality

\displaystyle K_{p}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{p}}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{j=2}^{m}\bigg\arrowvert\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)\beta_{1j}^{*}(z)r_{j}^{*\top}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}(z)r_{j}^{*}\bigg\arrowvert^{2}\bigg)^{p/2}

(B.54)

\displaystyle\leq K_{p}\frac{1}{m^{p/2}}\frac{2^{p}\arrowvert z\arrowvert^{p}}{\Im(z)^{p}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=2}^{m}\mathbb{E}\bigg\arrowvert r_{j}^{*\top}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}D_{1j}^{*}(z)^{-1}(z)r_{j}^{*}\bigg\arrowvert^{p}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\big(m^{-p/2}\big).

Combining (
B.53
) and (
B.54
) yields
(
B.52
).
Hence, we obtain for (
B.48
)
the bound
\mathcal{O}\big(m^{-1}+m/n)
, which proves (iii).

B.6
Proof of (
B.30
)

The proof follows the martingale arguments of the almost sure convergence of the random part for classical covariance matrices, replacing the expectations involved there by corresponding conditional expectations in the bootstrap world. We present the adapted reasoning for the sake of completeness.

Inserting and subtracting conditional expectations, we rewrite

\displaystyle m_{n}^{*}(z)-\mathbb{E}^{*}m_{n}^{*}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}\tr\big(D^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\tr\big(D^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)=:\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\rho_{j}^{*}.

Next, we get by conditional independence of
D^{*}_{j}(z)
from
X_{j}^{*}
, the Sherman-Morrison formula and invariance of the trace under cyclic permutation

\displaystyle\rho_{j}^{*}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)\Big[\tr\big(D^{*}(z)^{-1}\big)-\tr\big(D^{*}_{j}(z)^{-1}\big)\Big]=\big(\mathbb{E}_{j}^{*}-\mathbb{E}_{j-1}^{*}\big)\frac{r_{j}^{*\top}D^{*}_{j}(z)^{-2}r_{j}^{*}}{1+r_{j}^{*\top}D^{*}_{j}(z)^{-1}r_{j}^{*}}.

Moreover, with the diagonal representation
\sum_{j=1}^{m}r_{j}^{*}r_{j}^{*\top}=U\Lambda{U}^{\top}
for some diagonal matrix
\Lambda
and orthorgonal matrix
U
,

\displaystyle\bigg\arrowvert\frac{r_{j}^{*\top}D^{*}_{j}(z)^{-2}r_{j}^{*}}{1+r_{j}^{*\top}D^{*}_{j}(z)^{-1}r_{j}^{*}}\bigg\arrowvert

\displaystyle=\bigg\arrowvert\frac{({U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*})^{\top}(\Lambda-zI_{q})^{-2}{U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*}}{1+({U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*})^{\top}(\Lambda-zI_{q})^{-1}{U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*}}\bigg\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{({U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*})^{\top}((\Lambda-\Re(z)I_{q})^{2}+(\Im(z)I_{q})^{2})^{-1}{U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*}}{\Im(1+({U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*})^{\top}(\Lambda-zI_{q})^{-1}{U}^{\top}r_{j}^{*})}=\frac{1}{\Im(z)},

where we have used the identity

\Im\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda-z}\Big)=\frac{\Im(z)}{(\lambda-\Re(z))^{2}+\Im(z)^{2}}

for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
in the last identity. Therefore, the family
\rho_{1}^{*},\dots,\rho_{m}^{*}
forms a bounded martingale difference sequence, and writing the expectation
\mathbb{E}
as expected conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}\mathbb{E}^{*}
, an application of Burkholder’s inequality reveals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\arrowvert m_{n}^{*}(z)-\mathbb{E}^{*}m_{n}^{*}(z)\big\arrowvert^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K_{4}}{q^{4}}\mathbb{E}\Big(\mathbb{E}^{*}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\arrowvert\rho_{j}^{*}\arrowvert^{2}\Big)^{2}\leq\frac{4K_{4}m^{2}}{\Im(z)^{4}q^{4}}=\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{1}{m^{2}}\Big).

Because of

\frac{m}{q}\big(m_{n}^{*}(z)-\mathbb{E}^{*}m_{n}^{*}(z)\big)=\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)-\mathbb{E}^{*}\underline{m}_{n}^{*}(z)

the assertion (
B.30
) follows.

C
Proof of Theorem
4.3

We begin with the proof of Lemma
4.4
, which will be crucial for the proof of Theorem
4.3
.

For
r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
let
(x)_{r}=x(x-1)\ldots(x-r+1)
then

x^{r}=\sum^{r}_{j=0}a_{r,j}(x)_{j}

(C.1)

where
\{a_{r,j}\mid j=0,\ldots,r\}
are the Stirling numbers of the second kind
(
Riordan,, 1958
, see)
.
Using this representation twice we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod^{k}_{\ell=1}W^{s_{\ell}}_{\ell}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum^{s_{\ell}}_{j_{1}=0}\ldots\sum^{s_{k}}_{j_{k}=0}a_{s_{1},j_{1}}\ldots a_{s_{k},j_{k}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod^{k}_{\ell=1}(W_{\ell})_{j_{\ell}}\Big]

(C.2)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum^{s_{\ell}}_{j_{1}=0}\ldots\sum^{s_{k}}_{j_{k}=0}a_{s_{1},j_{1}}\ldots a_{s_{k},j_{k}}\prod^{k}_{\ell=1}\mathbb{E}\Big[(W_{\ell})_{j_{\ell}}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod^{k}_{\ell=1}\Big(\sum^{s_{\ell}}_{j_{\ell}=0}a_{s_{\ell},j_{\ell}}\mathbb{E}\Big[(W_{\ell})_{j_{\ell}}\Big]\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod^{k}_{\ell=1}\mathbb{E}\Big[W_{\ell}^{s_{\ell}}\Big]

where the inequality follows evaluating the factorial moments of the multinomial distribution,
which gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod^{k}_{\ell=1}(W_{\ell})_{j_{\ell}}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{m}\sum_{i_{1}\geq j_{1}\ldots i_{k}\geq j_{k}}\sum_{i_{k+1}\ldots i_{n}\geq 0}\frac{m!\mathds{1}\{\sum^{n}_{\ell=1}i_{\ell}=m\}}{\prod^{k}_{\ell=1}(i_{\ell}-j_{\ell})!i_{k+1}!\ldots i_{n}!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m!}{(m-j_{1}-\ldots-j_{k})!}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{m}n^{m-j_{1}-\ldots-j_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{m!}{(m-j_{\ell})!}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{m}n^{m-j_{\ell}}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\Big[(W_{\ell})_{j_{\ell}}\Big]~.

Therefore, the assertion will follow from the estimate for
s\geq 1

\max_{s\leq k_{m}}\Big(\frac{n}{m}\Big)\mathbb{E}[W^{s}_{\ell}]\leq 1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}~,

(C.3)

which we will prove in the following. For this purpose we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[(W_{\ell})_{k}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m!}{(m-k)!}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{m}n^{m-k}~\leq~\Big(\frac{m}{n}\Big)^{k}.

We now use (
C.1
) and obtain
for
s\geq 1
(note that
a_{s,0}=0
,
a_{s,1}=1
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[W^{s}_{\ell}\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum^{s}_{j=0}a_{s,j}\Big(\frac{m}{n}\Big)^{j}={m\over n}+a_{s,2}\Big({m\over n}\Big)^{2}+\Big({m\over n}\Big)^{2}R~,

(C.4)

where

\displaystyle R=\sum^{s}_{j=3}a_{s,j}\Big(\frac{m}{n}\Big)^{j-2}\leq\sum^{s}_{j=3}\Big({m\over n}\Big)^{j-2}s^{j}{j^{s}\over j!}

(C.5)

and we have used the estimate

a_{s,j}\leq\binom{s}{j}j^{s-j}\leq{s^{j}\over j!}j^{s}

for the Stirling numbers of the second kind.
We first show that the term in (
C.5
)
is bounded by a constant independently of
s
.
For this purpose we use the estimate
\log{m\over n}\leq-\log m
, for the terms in the sum in the sum

\displaystyle R_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big({m\over n}\Big)^{j-2}s^{j}{j^{s}\over j!}\leq\exp\{j\log s-(j-2)\log m+s\log j\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\{j\log k_{m}-(j-2)\log m+k_{m}\log j\}

Observing that
k_{m}=\lfloor\gamma\log m\rfloor
yields for all
m\geq m(\gamma)=e^{\gamma}

\displaystyle R_{j}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\{2j\log\log m-(j-2)\log m+\gamma\log m\log j\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp\Big\{\log m\Big(-{j\over 2}+2+\gamma\log j\Big)\Big\}~,

where the second inequality follows from
\log m\leq m^{1/4}
. We now define

j^{*}=j^{*}(\gamma)
as the smallest integer such that the inequality

\displaystyle\gamma\log j\leq{j\over 4}-{3\over 2}

(C.6)

holds for all
j\geq j^{*}
obtain

R_{j}\leq\Big({1\over{m}^{1/4}}\Big)^{j-2}

for all
j\geq j^{*}
. Consequently, for sufficiently large
m
the term

R\leq\sum_{j=3}^{j^{*}-1}\Big({m\over n}\Big)^{j-2}s^{j}{j^{s}\over j!}+\sum^{s}_{j=j^{*}}\Big({1\over{m}^{1/4}}\Big)^{j-2}

is bounded and we obtain from (
C.4
), observing that
a_{s,2}\leq 2^{s}
, that

\mathbb{E}\big[W^{s}_{\ell}\big]\leq\frac{m}{n}\Big\{1+c_{\gamma}\frac{m^{1+\gamma\log 2}}{n}\Big\}\leq\frac{m}{n}\Big\{1+c_{\gamma}\frac{m^{1+\gamma}}{n}\Big\}~,

where the bound is uniform with respect to
s\leq k_{m}
.
∎

Applying the same arguments as in Lemma 2.2 and 2.3 of

Yin et al., (1988)
and the reasoning at beginning of Section 2 in
Bai and Yin, (1993)

to the bootstrap matrix
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}
, we
may assume that
|X_{ij}|\leq\sqrt{m}\delta_{m}
for some sequence

\delta_{m}
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
D.1
as well as (
D.3
).

Next, we shall prove that
for the sequence
k_{m}=\lfloor\gamma\log m\rfloor

(with
\gamma>0
to be specified later) we have

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\frac{\Arrowvert\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}}{z}\Big)^{k_{m}}\Big]<\infty

(C.7)

for some
z>z_{0}(\gamma)>0
, where
z_{0}(\gamma)

will be specified later
according to the cases (a) and (b) in Theorem
4.3
.
In what follows, we suppress the
m
-dependence of
k=k_{m}
. Let
(W_{1},\cdots,W_{n})
denote a multinomial distributed vector with parameter
(m,(1/n,\cdots,1/n))
. Then (note that
\Sigma_{n}=I_{p}
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\Arrowvert\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\tr\Big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*k}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m^{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in\{1,\dots,q\}\\
j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\in\{1,\dots,n\}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\big[W_{j_{1}}\dots W_{j_{k}}\big]\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i_{1}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}j_{k}}X_{i_{1}j_{k}}\big].

The difference to the analysis of

Yin et al., (1988)

for the matrix
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}
are the additional factors
\mathbb{E}[W_{j_{1}}\dots W_{j_{k}}]
as well as the range of indices
\{1,\dots,q\},\{1,\dots,n\}
instead of
\{1,\dots,p\},\{1,\dots,n\}
in the above expression. Note that
n/p=\mathcal{O}(1)
while
n/q\rightarrow\infty
.

Nevertheless, due to the similarity of our expression to the corresponding expectation analyzed in
Yin et al., (1988)
, we may adopt their strategy of decomposing the summation as follows. Drawing two parallel lines, the so-called
I
-line and
J
-line, we can construct a directed multigraph by plotting for a given sequence
(i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2},\dots,i_{k},j_{k})
the indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in\{1,\dots,q\}
on the
I
-line, the indices
j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
on the
J
-line and interpret them as vertices on two disjoint classes on the two parallel lines. Edges will be the directed segments
i_{1}j_{1},j_{1}i_{2},\dots,j_{k}i_{1}
. They are
2k
in number and they are regarded as different from each other, even if they have the same initials and ends. Two edges are said to coincide if they have the same vertex set. If not every edge coincides at least with one other edge, then

\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i_{1}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}j_{k}}X_{i_{1}j_{k}}\big]=0.

In order to treat the remaining terms, we have to distinguish between different types of edges within canonical graphs, meaning graphs that satisfy
i_{1}=1
,
j_{1}=1
,
i_{k}\leq\max\{i_{k-1},\dots,i_{1}\}+1
and
j_{k}\leq\max\{i_{k-1},\dots,j_{1}\}+1
(
k\geq 2
). In the terminology of
Yin et al., (1988)
, an edge is called innovation if its right vertex does not occur before. Depending on whether the right vertex belongs to the
I
-line or
J
-line, it is called row- or column-innovation. An edge is called
T_{3}
-edge, if there is exactly one innovation before which coincides with it. An edge will be called
T_{4}
-edge, if it is neither an innovation nor
T_{3}
. Equipped with these notions, the remaining sum can be split into the sums
\sum^{\prime}\sum^{\prime\prime}\sum^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\tr\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*k}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m^{k}}\sum^{\prime}\sum^{\prime\prime}\sum^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathbb{E}[W_{j_{1}}\dots W_{j_{k}}]\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i_{1}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}j_{k}}X_{i_{1}j_{k}}\big].

Here, the
\sum^{\prime}
-summation is over different arrangement of the four different types of edges (row innovation, column innovation,
T_{3}
and
T_{4}
) at the
2k
positions, the
\sum^{\prime\prime}
-summation is running over different canonical graphs with given arrangement of the four types for
2k
positions, and the
\sum^{\prime\prime\prime}
-summation over those constellations for which the graph is isomorphic to the given canonical graph.

If each edge coincides at least with one other edge and if
r
denotes the number of row innovations and
l
the number of
T_{3}
-edges, then there are
l-r
column innovations and
(2k-2l)

T_{4}
-edges.
As shown in
Yin et al., (1988)

page 518 ff, the number of summands in the first sum is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{r=1}^{l}\binom{k}{r}\binom{k}{l-r}\binom{2k-l}{l},

the number of summands in the third sum can be estimated from above by

\displaystyle\sum^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\leq q^{r+1}n^{l-r}

if the canonical graph corresponding to
\sum^{\prime\prime\prime}
possesses
r
row innovations and
l

T_{3}
-edges, and finally, if
t
denotes the number of non-coincident
T_{4}
-edges,

\displaystyle\sum^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\leq k^{2t}(t+1)^{6k-6l},

where
t
ranges from
0
to
2k-2l
.

It remains to evaluate the corresponding summands

\mathbb{E}\big[W_{j_{1}}\dots W_{j_{k}}\big]\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i_{1}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}j_{k}}X_{i_{1}j_{k}}\big]

when there are
r
row innovations,
l

T_{3}
-edges and
t
non-coincident
T_{4}
-edges. As argued in

Yin et al., (1988)
,

\Big\arrowvert\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i_{1}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{1}}X_{i_{2}j_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}j_{k}}X_{i_{1}j_{k}}\big]\Big\arrowvert\leq k^{t}\big(\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\big)^{2k-2l-t},

while our Lemma
4.4
implies that

\Big\arrowvert\mathbb{E}\big[W_{j_{1}}\dots W_{j_{k}}\Big]\Big\arrowvert\leq\Big(\frac{m}{n}\Big)^{l-r}\Big(1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}\Big)^{k}

as there are
l-r
different indices ammong
j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}
.
Putting these ingredients together, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\tr(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*k})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m^{k}}\Big(1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}\Big)^{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{r=1}^{l}\bigg\{\binom{k}{r}\binom{k}{l-r}\binom{2k-l}{l}q^{r+1}n^{l-r}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\times\sum_{t=0}^{2k-2l}k^{2t}(t+1)^{6k-6l}K\Big(\frac{m}{n}\Big)^{l-r}k^{t}\big(\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\big)^{2k-2l-t}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq q\Big(1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}\Big)^{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{r=1}^{l}\binom{k}{r}\binom{k}{l-r}\binom{2k-l}{l}\Big(\frac{q}{m}\Big)^{r}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\times\sum_{t=0}^{2k-2l}k^{3t}(t+1)^{6k-6l}\big(\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\big)^{-t}\delta_{m}^{2(k-l)}.

Using now the same arguments as in
Yin et al., (1988)
,
pages 519 – 520 (replacing there
n
by
m
and
p
by
q
) we finally obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{\rm tr}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*k})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}\Big)^{k}\Big[\big(2mq\big)^{1/k}\big(1+\sqrt{\delta_{m}}\big)^{2}\Big\{\Big(1+\sqrt{q\over m}\Big)^{2}+\big({18\delta_{m}^{1/6}\gamma}\big)^{6}\Big\}\Big]^{k}

Note that
\big(1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}\big)^{k}\to 1
and

\big(1+\sqrt{\delta_{m}}\big)^{2}\big\{\big(1+\sqrt{q\over m}\big)^{2}+\big({18\delta_{m}^{1/6}\gamma}\big)^{6}\big\}\to(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
as
m\to\infty
.
Furthermore, if
m=o(\sqrt{n})
we use
\gamma=1/(1+\varepsilon/2)
for
\varepsilon>

to obtain

\displaystyle\big(2mq\big)^{1/k}\to e^{2+\varepsilon}

which proves (
C.7
) for any
z>z(\gamma)=e^{2}

in the case (a) of Theorem
4.3
.

If
m=o(\log n)
, we have

{m^{1+\gamma}\log m\over n}\to 0
for any
\gamma>0
, and it follows

\displaystyle\big(2mq\big)^{1/k}\to e^{2/\gamma}

for any
\gamma
. Therefore (
C.7
) holds for any
z>(1+\sqrt{c})^{2}
, which completes the proof of Theorem
4.3
(a) and (c).

For a proof of part (b), note that it
follows from the arguments in
Bai and Yin, (1993)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\Big(\lambda_{\min}\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)<K\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(\lambda_{\min}\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}-(1+c)I_{q}\big)<K-\big(1-\sqrt{c}\big)^{2}-2\sqrt{c}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\Arrowvert\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}-(1+c)I_{q}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}>2\sqrt{c}+\big(1-\sqrt{c}\big)^{2}-K\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\Arrowvert\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}-cI_{q}-\diag\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)\big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}>2\sqrt{c}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\big(1-\sqrt{c}\big)^{2}-K\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\Arrowvert\diag\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)-I_{q}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}>\frac{1}{2}\big(\big(1-\sqrt{c}\big)^{2}-K\big)\Big).

Hence, it remains to show that for any
\varepsilon>0
and any
l\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\Arrowvert\diag\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)-I_{q}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}>\varepsilon\Big)=o(m^{-l})

(C.8)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\Arrowvert\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}-\diag\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)-cI_{q}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}>2\sqrt{c}+\varepsilon\Big)=o(m^{-l}).

(C.9)

Proof of (
C.8
).
Since
q=\mathcal{O}(m)
, it is sufficient to show

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg\arrowvert\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Big(\arrowvert X_{i1}^{*}\arrowvert^{2}-1\Big)\bigg\arrowvert>\varepsilon\bigg)=o(m^{-l}).

(C.10)

For the sequence
k=k_{m}=\gamma\log m
,
an application of Markov’s inequality yields an upper bound on the left-hand side
(recall that
|X_{ij}|\leq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m})

\displaystyle m^{-2k}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Big(\arrowvert X_{i}^{*}\arrowvert^{2}-\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}\arrowvert^{2}\Big)\bigg]^{2k}

\displaystyle=m^{-2k}\varepsilon^{-2k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}\Big(\arrowvert X_{i}\arrowvert^{2}-\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}\arrowvert^{2}\Big)\bigg]^{2k}

\displaystyle=m^{-2k}\varepsilon^{-2k}{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\geq 0,\dots,i_{n}\geq 0\\
i_{1}+\dots+i_{n}=2k\end{subarray}}\binom{2k}{i_{1}\dots i_{n}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{t=1}^{n}W_{t}^{i_{t}}\Big(\arrowvert X_{i}\arrowvert^{2}-\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}\arrowvert^{2}\Big)^{i_{t}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{2k}m^{-2k}\varepsilon^{-2k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\binom{n}{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\geq 2,\dots,i_{l}\geq 2\\
i_{1}+\dots+i_{l}=2k\end{subarray}}\binom{2k}{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{t=1}^{l}W_{t}^{i_{t}}\bigg]\prod_{t=1}^{l}\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}\arrowvert^{2i_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq K2^{2k}m^{-2k}\varepsilon^{-2k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}n^{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\geq 2,\dots,i_{l}\geq 2\\
i_{1}+\dots+i_{l}=2k\end{subarray}}\binom{2k}{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}\Big(\frac{m}{n}\Big)^{l}\Big(\ 1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}\Big)^{k}\prod_{t=1}^{l}\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}\arrowvert^{2i_{t}}

\displaystyle=K2^{2k}m^{-2k}\varepsilon^{-2k}\Big(\ 1+c_{\gamma}{m^{1+\gamma}\over n}\Big)^{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}m^{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\geq 2,\dots,i_{l}\geq 2\\
i_{1}+\dots+i_{l}=2k\end{subarray}}\binom{2k}{i_{1}\dots i_{l}}\prod_{t=1}^{l}\mathbb{E}\arrowvert X_{1}\arrowvert^{2i_{t}},

where Lemma
4.4
has been applied in the last inequality.
Arguing as in the proof of Lemma 2’ in

Bai and Silverstein, (2004)
, page 602 (where
f=1
and
their
m
corresponds to
k
), we obtain the upper bound

\displaystyle\gamma\log m\Big({16\gamma\delta_{m}^{2}\log m\over\varepsilon\log(4\delta_{m}^{4}m/\mathbb{E}|X_{11}|^{4}))}\Big)^{2\gamma\log m}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma(\log m)\Big({32\gamma\delta_{m}^{2}\over\varepsilon}\Big)^{2\gamma\log m}

\displaystyle=\gamma(\log m)m^{2\gamma\log\big({32\gamma\delta_{m}^{2}\over\varepsilon}\big)}

(C.11)

if
m
is sufficiently large such that

\log(4\delta_{m}^{4}m/\mathbb{E}|X_{11}|^{4})\geq{1\over 2}\log m
(note that by (
D.3
) eventually,
\delta_{m}\geq m^{-1/8}
as
m\to\infty
).
Because of

\delta_{m}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
, it follows that for any
a\in(0,1)
, there exists an integer

m_{0}=m_{0}(a)
such that the expression in (
C.11
) is bounded by
m^{2\gamma\log a}
for all

m\geq m_{0}
, which proves (
C.10
) and completes the proof of (
C.8
).

Proof of (
C.9
).
With the notation
\hat{T}_{n}^{*}=\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}-\diag\big(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)
, it is sufficient to prove the following result. There exists a positive constant
C>0
, such that for every
r\in\mathbb{N}
and positive
\varepsilon
and
l
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\big\Arrowvert\hat{T}_{n}^{*}-cI_{q}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{r}>Cr^{4}2^{r}c^{r/2}+\varepsilon\Big)=o(m^{-l}).

(C.12)

To this aim, we need to establish the bootstrap analogs of lemmata 1’ – 8’ in the appendix of
Bai and Silverstein, (2004)
. Since all of them can be deduced by our manipulation technique and Lemma
4.4
in a straightforward manner, we omit them at this point.

∎

We begin part (b). By the discussion in Section
B.1
we can assume that
R_{n}=0
, which
gives

\displaystyle{x^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}x\over\|x\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\rm min}\Big({1\over m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{*}X_{i}^{*\top}\Big){x^{\top}L_{n}L_{n}^{\top}x\over\|x\|^{2}}\geq\lambda_{\rm min}\Big({1\over m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{*}X_{i}^{*\top}\Big)\lambda_{\rm min}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}).

The assertion follows applying Theorem
4.3
for
q^{\prime}\times q^{\prime}
-matrix
{1\over m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}^{*}X_{i}^{*\top}
and using the inequality
\lambda_{\rm min}(\tilde{\Sigma}_{n})\geq\lambda_{\rm min}(\Sigma_{n})
.
Part (a) is an immediate consequence of the fact that the spectral norm is a matrix norm.
∎

D
Proof of Theorem
4.6

Throughout this section, we assume that Assumptions (A1) – (A3+) are satisfied.
All proofs have in common the truncation steps in (
D.1
) and (
D.8
) discussed in the following Section
D.1
and
D.2
, respectively.

D.1
Reduction to
L_{n}

Recalling the notation from
Section
B.1
we will will first prove that we can replace the matrix
\Pi_{n}A_{n}
in the decomposition (
3.2
)
by the matrix
L_{n}
, that is

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{n}^{*}(f)-\hat{T}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}(f)\rightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0

(D.1)

where

\hat{T}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}(f)

denotes the linear spectral statistics corresponding to the matrix

\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}:=\frac{1}{m}L_{n}X^{*}X^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top},

and
L_{n}X^{*}=(L_{n}X^{*}_{1},\ldots,L_{n}X^{*}_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times m}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}=\Big\{\lambda_{\min}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*})>K_{\rm left},~\lambda_{\min}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,L_{n}}^{*})>K_{\rm left},~\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}\|_{S_{\infty}}<K_{\rm right},~\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\|_{S_{\infty}}<K_{\rm right}\Big\}

where constants
K_{\rm left}
and
K_{\rm right}
come from Corollary
4.5
. By this result we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{D}_{n}^{c})=o(m^{-\ell})
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
(note that due to the Representative Subpopulation Condition
3.1
, it follows that

\big|\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}\|_{S_{\infty}}-\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\|_{S_{\infty}}\big|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1))
.
By the Lipschitz continuity of
f
, the
1
-Wielandt-Hoffman inequality, and Assumption (A1), it follows that

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert\hat{T}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}(f)-\hat{T}_{n}^{*}(f)\big\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq{\max}_{\lambda\in[K_{\rm left},K_{\rm right}]}|f^{\prime}(\lambda)|\sum_{j=1}^{q}\big\arrowvert{\hat{\lambda}}_{j,L_{n}}^{*}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{*}\arrowvert+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\leq{\max}_{\lambda\in[K_{\rm left},K_{\rm right}]}|f^{\prime}(\lambda)|\big\Arrowvert\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}-\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big\Arrowvert_{S_{1}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)~,

\hat{\lambda}_{j,L_{n}}^{*}
is the
j
th eigenvalue of the matrix
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n,L_{n}}^{*}
. By the discussion in Section
B.1
, the right-hand side is of order
o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
if

\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{n}}\big[||R_{n}||_{S_{2}}^{2}\big]=o(1)
, which proves (
D.1
).

Therefore, we will assume in the following discussion
that given the random projection
\Pi_{n}

the matrix
\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}
can be represented as

\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}={1\over m}L_{n}X^{*}X^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top}~,

where
L_{n}
is a
q\times q^{\prime}
matrix
satisfying
\|L_{n}\|_{S_{\infty}}\leq\alpha<\infty
(for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
)
and
X^{*}
is an
q^{\prime}\times m
matrix and
q^{\prime}=O(q)
.
Note that these arguments only require the existence of moments of order
4
.

D.2
Reduction to truncated components

We will continue truncating the random variables
X_{ij}
. For this purpose we formulate the following lemma.

Lemma D.1
.

There exists a sequence
(\delta_{m})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converging decreasingly to zero such that

\displaystyle\delta_{m}^{-4}\mathbb{E}\Big(I_{\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert\geq\delta_{m}m^{1/2}\}}X_{11}^{4}\Big)\longrightarrow 0\ \ \text{as}\ \ n\rightarrow\infty.

(D.2)

The proof of (
D.2
) is given on page 559 in
Bai and Silverstein, (2004)
, that we repeat here for the reader’s convenience. First observe that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a strictly increasing sequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with

k^{4}\mathbb{E}\Big(I\big\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert\geq m_{n_{k}}^{1/2}/k\big\}X_{11}^{4}\Big)<\frac{1}{2^{k}}

by monotone convergence, because
\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{4}<\infty
. Choose
\delta_{m}=1/k
for
n\in[n_{k},n_{k+1})
,
\delta_{m}=1
for
n<n_{1}
. Then,
\delta_{m}\searrow 0
and

\delta_{m}^{-4}\mathbb{E}\Big(I_{\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert\geq\delta_{m}m^{1/2}\}}X_{11}^{4}\Big)\longrightarrow 0\ \ \text{as}\ \ n\rightarrow\infty.

∎

We choose the sequence
(\delta_{m})

such that Lemma
D.1
holds and additionally
such that

\displaystyle\delta_{m}m^{1/8}\rightarrow\infty~.

(D.3)

With this sequence, we
show that it is sufficient
to consider random variables which satisfy

\displaystyle\arrowvert X_{ij}\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\ \ i=1,\dots n,\ j=1,\dots,p

(D.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\ X_{11}

\displaystyle=0

(D.5)

\displaystyle\Var(X_{11})

\displaystyle=1

(D.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}X_{11}^{4}

\displaystyle\rightarrow 3.

(D.7)

For this purpose, we introduce the notation

\displaystyle\check{X}_{ij}=\frac{X_{ij}I\big\{|X_{ij}|\leq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\big\}-\mathbb{E}\big[X_{ij}I\big\{|X_{ij}|\leq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\big\}\big]}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}\big(X_{ij}I\big\{|X_{ij}|\leq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\big\}\big)}}.

We write

\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}L_{n}\check{X}_{i}^{*}{\check{X}^{*\top}_{i}}L_{n}^{\top}

and denote by

\check{\lambda}_{i}^{*}
(
i=1,\dots,q
) its eigenvalues in decreasing order and by

\check{T}_{n}^{*}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{q}f(\check{\lambda}_{i}^{*})

the corresponding linear spectral statistic.

Lemma D.2
(Bootstrap truncation lemma).

Grant Assumptions (A1)–(A3+). Then

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{n}^{*}(f)-\check{T}_{n}^{*}(f)\rightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0.

(D.8)

For the sequence
(\delta_{m})
specified above we
set

\tilde{X}_{ij}:=X_{ij}I\{\arrowvert X_{ij}\arrowvert<\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\}

and denote by
\tilde{X}_{1}^{*},\dots,\tilde{X}_{n}^{*}
the corresponding iid sample from
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\hat{X}_{i}}
. With

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}Y_{i}^{*}Y_{i}^{*\top}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}L_{n}{X}_{i}^{*}{X}_{i}^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top}

(D.9)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}L_{n}\tilde{X}_{i}^{*}{\tilde{X}_{i}^{*\top}}L_{n}^{\top}

(D.10)

we get by the union bound and Lemma
D.1
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\not=\tilde{B}_{n}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\tilde{X}_{ij}^{*}\not=X_{ij}^{*}\text{ for some }(i,j)\Big)\leq mq^{\prime}\,\mathbb{P}\big(\tilde{X}_{11}^{*}\not={X}_{11}^{*}\big)

(D.11)

\displaystyle=mq^{\prime}\,\mathbb{E}\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}I\{\arrowvert X_{i1}\arrowvert\geq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\}\Big)=mq^{\prime}\,\mathbb{P}(\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert>\delta_{m}\sqrt{m})

\displaystyle\leq K\delta_{m}^{-4}\int_{\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert\geq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\}}\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert^{4}d\mathbb{P}=o(1).

Now, we are passing over
from
\tilde{X}_{1},\dots,\tilde{X}_{n}

to the centered and standardized modifications

\check{X}_{1},\dots,\check{X}_{n}
, where

\check{X}_{ij}:=\frac{\tilde{X}_{ij}-\mathbb{E}\tilde{X}_{ij}}{\sigma_{n}}\ \ \text{ with }\sigma_{n}:=\sqrt{\Var(\tilde{X}_{ij})},

and denote by
\check{X}_{1}^{*},\dots,\check{X}_{m}^{*}
the corresponding iid sample from
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\tilde{X}_{i}}
.
Further, we introduce

\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}=(\check{X}_{1}^{*},\dots,\check{X}_{m}^{*})
and
{\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}}^{*}=(\tilde{X}_{1}^{*},\dots,\tilde{X}_{m}^{*})
,

\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}L_{n}\check{X}_{i}^{*}\check{X}_{i}^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top}=L_{n}\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top},

and denote by
\check{\lambda}_{1}^{*}\geq\check{\lambda}_{2}^{*}\geq\dots\geq\check{\lambda}_{q}^{*}
its eigenvalues
with corresponding linear spectral statistic

\check{T}_{n}^{*}(f)=\sum_{j=1}^{q}f(\check{\lambda}_{j}).

By employing the Lipschitz continuity of
f
, the
1
-Wielandt-Hoffman inequality and the reasoning of the proof of Lemma 2.7 in

Bai, (1999)
, we deduce the upper bound

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert\check{T}_{n}^{*}(f)-\tilde{T}_{n}^{*}(f)\big\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq{\max}_{\lambda\in[K_{\rm left},K_{\rm right}]}|f^{\prime}(\lambda)|\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{q}\big\arrowvert\check{\lambda}_{j}^{*}-\tilde{\lambda}_{j}^{*}\arrowvert+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

\displaystyle\leq{2}{\max}_{\lambda\in[K_{\rm left},K_{\rm right}]}|f^{\prime}(\lambda)|\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{m}\tr\Big[L_{n}({\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}}-\check{\mathfrak{X}})({\mathfrak{X}}-\check{\mathfrak{X}})^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\Big]\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\Big(\tr\big(\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}\big)+\tr\big(\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)\Big)^{1/2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

(D.12)

where
\tilde{T}_{n}^{*}(f)=\sum_{j=1}^{q}f(\tilde{\lambda}_{j})
denotes the linear spectral statistic corresponding to the matrix
\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}=L_{n}\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{*}\check{\mathfrak{X}}^{*\top}L_{n}^{\top}
.
In order to bound the latter expression, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}

\displaystyle\tr\Big[L_{n}(\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}-\check{\mathfrak{X}})(\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}-\check{\mathfrak{X}})^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{m}\Big(1-\frac{1}{\sigma_{n}}\Big)^{2}q\Arrowvert\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}+\frac{2}{m}\frac{1}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}\tr\left[L_{n}(\mathbb{E}\tilde{X}_{1})(\mathbb{E}\tilde{X}_{1})^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2c\frac{(\sigma_{n}^{2}-1)^{2}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}(1+\sigma_{n})^{2}}\Arrowvert\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}+\frac{2c}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}(\mathbb{E}\tilde{X}_{11})^{2}\Arrowvert L_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}.

But

\displaystyle\arrowvert\sigma_{n}^{2}-1\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\Big(I\{\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert\geq\delta_{m}\sqrt{m}\}\arrowvert X_{11}\arrowvert^{2}\Big)=o(\delta_{m}^{2}m^{-1})

and

\displaystyle\arrowvert\mathbb{E}\tilde{X}_{11}\arrowvert

\displaystyle=o(\delta_{m}m^{-3/2}),

(D.13)

such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}

\displaystyle\tr\Big[L_{n}(\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}-\check{\mathfrak{X}})(\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}-\check{\mathfrak{X}})^{\top}L_{n}^{\top}\Big]=o(\delta_{m}^{4}m^{-2})\Arrowvert\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}+o(\delta_{m}^{2}m^{-3})\Arrowvert L_{n}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}^{2}.

Plugging this bound into (
D.12
), we find

\displaystyle\big\arrowvert\check{T}_{n}^{*}(f)-\tilde{T}_{n}^{*}(f)\big\arrowvert

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(1)o\big(\delta_{m}^{2}m^{-1}\sqrt{\Arrowvert\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}\Arrowvert_{S_{\infty}}}+o(\delta_{m}m^{-3/2})\big)\Big)O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{q}),

(D.14)

where we used the fact that, by Theorem
4.3
and (
D.11
),

{\max}_{\lambda\in[K_{\rm left},K_{\rm right}]}|f^{\prime}(\lambda)|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
and

\displaystyle\tr\big(\tilde{B}_{n}^{*}\big)+\tr\big(\check{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\big)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}({q}).

This proves (
D.8
). Note that these arguments only require the existence of the moments order
4
.
∎

Summarizing the discussion of Section
D.1
and
D.2
, we will from now assume that the random variables
X_{ij}

satisfy (
D.4
) - (
D.7
), that the vectors
X_{i}
have
q^{\prime}=O(q)
components and that the matrix
L_{n}
is of dimension
q\times q^{\prime}
.
Note that the matrix
L_{n}
can be a random matrix which is independent of
X_{1},\ldots X_{n}
.

D.3
Passing over to the bootstrapped process of Stieltjes transforms

Define

\displaystyle{M}_{n}^{*}(z)

\displaystyle:=q\big(m_{\mu^{\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}}}(z)-m_{n}^{0}(z)\big),

where
m_{n}^{0}(z)
denotes the Stieltjes transform of the measure
\mu_{p/n,{\mu^{\Sigma_{n}}}}^{0}
. Moreover,

\mathcal{D}_{n}=\big\{\lambda_{\min}(\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*})>K_{\rm left}~,~\|\widehat{\Sigma}_{n}^{*}\|_{S_{\infty}}<K_{\rm right}\big\}.

By the relation

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{n}^{*}(f)-q\int fd\mu_{p/n,{\mu^{\Sigma_{n}}}}^{0}=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\ointctrclockwise f(z){M}_{n}^{*}(z)\dd z

(D.15)

provided by the Cauchy integral formula, it follows from Corollary
4.5
that the result is deduced from the corresponding limit theorem for

\ointctrclockwise f(z){M}_{n}^{*}(z)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\dd z,

where the curve integral is along any closed curve within a
region on which
f
is analytic and which encloses the interval
[K_{\rm left},K_{\rm right}]
. The latter indeed boils down to proving a conditional Donsker-type theorem of a truncated version of the bootstrapped process
M_{n}^{*}(.)
, denoted by
\widehat{M}_{n}^{*}(.)
,
see Section
E.3
. For a precise definition of
\widehat{M}_{n}^{*}(.)
let
x_{l}
,
x_{r}
be two real numbers with

\displaystyle x_{l}\in\begin{cases}(0,K_{\rm left})&\text{if }c^{\prime}\in(0,1)\\
(-\infty,~0)&\text{if }c^{\prime}\geq 1\end{cases}

and
x_{r}>K_{\rm right}
, where
K_{\rm left}
and
K_{\rm right}
are the constants introduced in Corollary
4.5
and

c^{\prime}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{q^{\prime}}{m}.

Moreover, define

\mathcal{C}_{u}=\{x+iv_{0}:~x\in[x_{l},x_{r}]\}

and
